But now I look and look before I settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I keep making excuses not to go down to the senior center much anymore. They have cards on Tuesdays, and I used to like that, but now I’ll tell myself the weather’s no good or I’ve got something at home.\n\nAnd the garage, too… I don’t like working out there lately. Especially near the tools, and that corner where I used to keep the ladder. I’ll go out there meaning to fix something, then I end up just standing there, or I turn around and come back in. Funny, I’ve got jars of old screws out there from twenty years ago, all sorted by size… but I can’t seem to make myself stay in there long now.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific and upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that you think might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Yes… it was the ladder accident. That’s what I keep coming back to. It’s been going on since then, about… oh, a year and seven months now, I’d say.\n\nI remember I used to keep that ladder in the garage corner, and I was always telling myself I ought to replace the little rubber feet on it. You put things off, you know, and then time just gets away from you. But yes… that accident, that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of using the ladder again that makes you avoid the garage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not just using it, exactly. It’s that whole corner of the garage. The tools are there, and that’s where I used to keep the ladder, and I’ll find some reason not to go in.\n\nSame with the senior center sometimes—I’ll say I’m tired or it might rain, anything really. Funny, they probably still have my seat at the card table, unless old Pete took it. But yes… I keep avoiding those places more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, doctor… “reached a peak,” I don’t know. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get real uneasy and want to get away from where I am, but whether it comes on all at once like that… I can’t say.\n\nSometimes I get to thinking too much, and then I notice I’m standing there doing nothing. Like the other day I went to look for a wrench and ended up sorting through an old coffee can full of bolts instead—those Folgers cans last forever, you know. But as far as a sudden big rush of fear in just a few minutes… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that came on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say overwhelming, exactly. Sudden, maybe now and then I get a start, but… I’m not sure it’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing somewhere and feel like I ought to leave, but then I get thinking about something else—like whether I locked the back door, or where I put my glasses. I do that a lot, put them down on the newspaper and then blame the newspaper.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I’ve had that kind of big sudden fear you’re asking about. Not that I’ve noticed clear enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the ladder accident as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… not like I’m dreaming it, but if I hear metal scrape—like a ladder or somebody dragging tools—or I see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, it sort of jumps right back into my head.\n\nFor a second it feels like I’m under there again, waiting for somebody to come help. Then I’ll look around and remember where I am. Funny how a sound can do that… my neighbor’s got this old aluminum ladder he leaves out by the fence, and I wish he’d put the thing away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid thinking about the ladder accident or try to push memories of it out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t sit down and think, “I’m not gonna think about it,” but I keep busy with other things. I’ll find some reason not to go over to the senior center, or not to mess around in the garage.\n\nThe tools are out there, and that corner where I used to keep the ladder… well, I just don’t care to be near it much. I’ll tell myself I need to check the mail, or make coffee, or look for something in the kitchen. Funny thing, half the time I forget what I went in there for. But yes, I suppose I do push it away some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the ladder accident as if it were happening all over again, without being triggered by a specific sound or sight?\n\nPatient: Not really just out of nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more when something sets it off, like that metal scraping sound, or seeing a ladder propped up in somebody’s yard.\n\nThen, for a moment, the whole thing comes back like I’m under there again, waiting for someone to help. But if there’s no sound or nothing I see… I don’t know, I can’t say it happens that way. Funny, though, how just one little scrape can do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know folks say things like that, like they’re looking down at themselves, but that’s not really how it is for me.\n\nIt’s more like I get startled and my mind goes back for a second, then I’m standing there in my kitchen or by the fence or wherever. I’m still me, still in my body, far as I can tell. Though, you know, my daughter once told me I looked pale as a sheet when I got spooked by a noise—funny expression, pale as a sheet. My mother used to say that all the time. But no, I haven’t noticed watching myself from outside or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or the way you've been feeling affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I suppose. My wife will be sitting right there next to me, and the fellows I play bridge with will be cutting up, telling jokes, you know how men do when they’ve known each other a while… and I feel like I’m behind a window or something.\n\nI can see them, hear them, but I don’t feel quite… with them. Like I’m there at the table, but not really part of it. Course, bridge is funny that way too, because if you miss a bid they’ll let you know about it—my friend Carl, he never lets a man forget a bad hand. But yes, I think it’s made me pull back some, even when I’m sitting right there with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or the world around you when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Detached from myself, or the world… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just quiet, sitting there, and my wife says I’m staring off, but I might just be thinking about whether I left the garage light on or what we need from the store.\n\nDetached is a funny word. Makes me think of when a trailer hitch isn’t set right—my brother-in-law had one come loose years ago, scared him half to death. But no, when I’m not thinking about the ladder thing, I can’t say I notice feeling separate from everything. I’m just… there, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from what's happening around you, even when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: A daze… well, I don’t know. I mean, I get tired sometimes, but at my age who doesn’t, right? My wife’ll ask me something when the television’s on and I might miss it, but that could just be my hearing. She says I keep the volume up too high, same as my father did.\n\nBut disconnected from what’s going on when I’m not thinking about that accident… no, I can’t say I’ve really noticed that. If I’m sitting there quiet, I’m usually just sitting there quiet. Maybe thinking on some little thing, like whether I shut the gate or what’s for supper. So… no, not in a way I’d call being in a daze.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities since the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t work anymore, not regular work anyhow. Haven’t for a while. But around the house… yes, I suppose things have changed some.\n\nI still get up, wash, eat breakfast, do my usual things. I’m not helpless. But there are chores I used to just do without thinking, and now I kind of steer around them, and then my wife ends up doing it. I don’t like that. She’s got enough on her plate.\n\nAnd I used to go down to the senior center more, play cards, talk with the fellas. They had terrible coffee there—tasted like it sat on the burner since Eisenhower—but I went. Lately I just don’t much. Same with my bridge friends. I don’t visit like I used to. So the days are… smaller, I guess. More just here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have interfered with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Significant… well, I guess it depends what you call significant. I still get up and do my regular things, you know, wash up, have breakfast, putter around. I’m not just sitting in a chair all day like my uncle did after his hip went bad—though he was stubborn as a mule about that walker.\n\nBut yes, it’s changed things some. I don’t go over to the senior center like I used to, and I hardly see the bridge fellas anymore. Around the house, there are things I avoid, and then my wife ends up doing chores that used to be mine. I don’t feel too good about that.\n\nSo… I suppose my days have gotten smaller. More just me at home, and her picking up some of what I don’t do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently avoiding thoughts, conversations, or memories related to the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I don’t sit there saying to myself, “Don’t think about it,” exactly. It’s more like I find some other reason not to get near it.\n\nLike the senior center—I keep meaning to go, then I say, ah, it’s too cold, or I don’t feel like hearing all the chatter today. And the garage too. I’ll tell myself it needs tidying but then I won’t go work around in there, especially where the tools are, and that spot where I used to keep the ladder.\n\nFunny thing, I still know where every wrench is supposed to go. My father had a pegboard in his shed, and if you put the pliers back wrong, he’d notice before supper. But… anyway, yes. I guess I do avoid things that bring it back. Not all the time, but enough that I notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I’ve had moments where I get real uneasy and want to get away from where I am, but whether it comes on all at once like that… I can’t say.\n\nSometimes I get to thinking too much, and then I notice I’m standing there doing nothing. Like the other day I went to look for a wrench and ended up sorting through an old coffee can full of bolts instead—those Folgers cans last forever, you know. But as far as a sudden big rush of fear in just a few minutes… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that came on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say overwhelming, exactly. Sudden, maybe now and then I get a start, but… I’m not sure it’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing somewhere and feel like I ought to leave, but then I get thinking about something else—like whether I locked the back door, or where I put my glasses. I do that a lot, put them down on the newspaper and then blame the newspaper.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I’ve had that kind of big sudden fear you’re asking about. Not that I’ve noticed clear enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the ladder accident as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… not like I’m dreaming it, but if I hear metal scrape—like a ladder or somebody dragging tools—or I see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, it sort of jumps right back into my head.\n\nFor a second it feels like I’m under there again, waiting for somebody to come help. Then I’ll look around and remember where I am. Funny how a sound can do that… my neighbor’s got this old aluminum ladder he leaves out by the fence, and I wish he’d put the thing away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid thinking about the ladder accident or try to push memories of it out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t sit down and think, “I’m not gonna think about it,” but I keep busy with other things. I’ll find some reason not to go over to the senior center, or not to mess around in the garage.\n\nThe tools are out there, and that corner where I used to keep the ladder… well, I just don’t care to be near it much. I’ll tell myself I need to check the mail, or make coffee, or look for something in the kitchen. Funny thing, half the time I forget what I went in there for. But yes, I suppose I do push it away some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the ladder accident as if it were happening all over again, without being triggered by a specific sound or sight?\n\nPatient: Not really just out of nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more when something sets it off, like that metal scraping sound, or seeing a ladder propped up in somebody’s yard.\n\nThen, for a moment, the whole thing comes back like I’m under there again, waiting for someone to help. But if there’s no sound or nothing I see… I don’t know, I can’t say it happens that way. Funny, though, how just one little scrape can do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know folks say things like that, like they’re looking down at themselves, but that’s not really how it is for me.\n\nIt’s more like I get startled and my mind goes back for a second, then I’m standing there in my kitchen or by the fence or wherever. I’m still me, still in my body, far as I can tell. Though, you know, my daughter once told me I looked pale as a sheet when I got spooked by a noise—funny expression, pale as a sheet. My mother used to say that all the time. But no, I haven’t noticed watching myself from outside or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or the way you've been feeling affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I suppose. My wife will be sitting right there next to me, and the fellows I play bridge with will be cutting up, telling jokes, you know how men do when they’ve known each other a while… and I feel like I’m behind a window or something.\n\nI can see them, hear them, but I don’t feel quite… with them. Like I’m there at the table, but not really part of it. Course, bridge is funny that way too, because if you miss a bid they’ll let you know about it—my friend Carl, he never lets a man forget a bad hand. But yes, I think it’s made me pull back some, even when I’m sitting right there with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or the world around you when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Detached from myself, or the world… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just quiet, sitting there, and my wife says I’m staring off, but I might just be thinking about whether I left the garage light on or what we need from the store.\n\nDetached is a funny word. Makes me think of when a trailer hitch isn’t set right—my brother-in-law had one come loose years ago, scared him half to death. But no, when I’m not thinking about the ladder thing, I can’t say I notice feeling separate from everything. I’m just… there, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from what's happening around you, even when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: A daze… well, I don’t know. I mean, I get tired sometimes, but at my age who doesn’t, right? My wife’ll ask me something when the television’s on and I might miss it, but that could just be my hearing. She says I keep the volume up too high, same as my father did.\n\nBut disconnected from what’s going on when I’m not thinking about that accident… no, I can’t say I’ve really noticed that. If I’m sitting there quiet, I’m usually just sitting there quiet. Maybe thinking on some little thing, like whether I shut the gate or what’s for supper. So… no, not in a way I’d call being in a daze.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities since the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t work anymore, not regular work anyhow. Haven’t for a while. But around the house… yes, I suppose things have changed some.\n\nI still get up, wash, eat breakfast, do my usual things. I’m not helpless. But there are chores I used to just do without thinking, and now I kind of steer around them, and then my wife ends up doing it. I don’t like that. She’s got enough on her plate.\n\nAnd I used to go down to the senior center more, play cards, talk with the fellas. They had terrible coffee there—tasted like it sat on the burner since Eisenhower—but I went. Lately I just don’t much. Same with my bridge friends. I don’t visit like I used to. So the days are… smaller, I guess. More just here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have interfered with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Significant… well, I guess it depends what you call significant. I still get up and do my regular things, you know, wash up, have breakfast, putter around. I’m not just sitting in a chair all day like my uncle did after his hip went bad—though he was stubborn as a mule about that walker.\n\nBut yes, it’s changed things some. I don’t go over to the senior center like I used to, and I hardly see the bridge fellas anymore. Around the house, there are things I avoid, and then my wife ends up doing chores that used to be mine. I don’t feel too good about that.\n\nSo… I suppose my days have gotten smaller. More just me at home, and her picking up some of what I don’t do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently avoiding thoughts, conversations, or memories related to the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I don’t sit there saying to myself, “Don’t think about it,” exactly. It’s more like I find some other reason not to get near it.\n\nLike the senior center—I keep meaning to go, then I say, ah, it’s too cold, or I don’t feel like hearing all the chatter today. And the garage too. I’ll tell myself it needs tidying but then I won’t go work around in there, especially where the tools are, and that spot where I used to keep the ladder.\n\nFunny thing, I still know where every wrench is supposed to go. My father had a pegboard in his shed, and if you put the pliers back wrong, he’d notice before supper. But… anyway, yes. I guess I do avoid things that bring it back. Not all the time, but enough that I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thoughts or memories about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I guess I do. I don’t always call it that in my head, but I keep finding reasons not to go over to the senior center, or not to work around the garage.\n\nThe garage especially… near the tools and that spot where I used to keep the ladder. I’ll say, “Well, I’ll do it tomorrow,” or “It’s too much mess today.” Funny, I used to keep that place pretty tidy—had coffee cans full of screws, all sorted out. But now I just sort of… stay away from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… a lot of distress, I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. I mean, I still get myself washed up, eat breakfast, do my little routine. I’m not helpless.

But it has interfered, yes. I don’t go to the senior center like I did, and I hardly see the bridge fellows anymore. We used to sit there for hours arguing over bids—one man, Harold, always thought he was smarter than the rest of us. Anyway… I miss that more than I admit.

And around the house, I avoid enough things that my wife ends up doing chores I used to handle. That bothers me. So I guess my life’s gotten smaller, more just staying home and letting things slide.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleep, doctor. I don’t sleep right anymore. I lay there a long time, just staring, and then when I do drift off, I wake up from bad dreams… or even just little noises. Then I can’t get myself settled back down again. It wears on you, you know. My old clock in the room, it ticks louder at night than it ever did in the daytime, funny how that is… but, uh, yes, that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I do. Not like I’m shaking all day, but when I go places now, I’m always looking around first. I check what might fall, where the exits are, if the floor looks uneven or the steps look steady before I can sit easy.\n\nFunny thing, I never used to notice floors much. My wife used to say I could trip over a church pew and blame the carpet. But now I look and look before I settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because of your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I keep making excuses not to go down to the senior center much anymore. They have cards on Tuesdays, and I used to like that, but now I’ll tell myself the weather’s no good or I’ve got something at home.\n\nAnd the garage, too… I don’t like working out there lately. Especially near the tools, and that corner where I used to keep the ladder. I’ll go out there meaning to fix something, then I end up just standing there, or I turn around and come back in. Funny, I’ve got jars of old screws out there from twenty years ago, all sorted by size… but I can’t seem to make myself stay in there long now.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific and upsetting happened to you recently or in the past that you think might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Yes… it was the ladder accident. That’s what I keep coming back to. It’s been going on since then, about… oh, a year and seven months now, I’d say.\n\nI remember I used to keep that ladder in the garage corner, and I was always telling myself I ought to replace the little rubber feet on it. You put things off, you know, and then time just gets away from you. But yes… that accident, that’s when all this seemed to start.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of using the ladder again that makes you avoid the garage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not just using it, exactly. It’s that whole corner of the garage. The tools are there, and that’s where I used to keep the ladder, and I’ll find some reason not to go in.\n\nSame with the senior center sometimes—I’ll say I’m tired or it might rain, anything really. Funny, they probably still have my seat at the card table, unless old Pete took it. But yes… I keep avoiding those places more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that, doctor… “reached a peak,” I don’t know. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get real uneasy and want to get away from where I am, but whether it comes on all at once like that… I can’t say.\n\nSometimes I get to thinking too much, and then I notice I’m standing there doing nothing. Like the other day I went to look for a wrench and ended up sorting through an old coffee can full of bolts instead—those Folgers cans last forever, you know. But as far as a sudden big rush of fear in just a few minutes… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you felt an overwhelming fear or discomfort that came on very suddenly?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say overwhelming, exactly. Sudden, maybe now and then I get a start, but… I’m not sure it’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing somewhere and feel like I ought to leave, but then I get thinking about something else—like whether I locked the back door, or where I put my glasses. I do that a lot, put them down on the newspaper and then blame the newspaper.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I’ve had that kind of big sudden fear you’re asking about. Not that I’ve noticed clear enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the ladder accident as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… not like I’m dreaming it, but if I hear metal scrape—like a ladder or somebody dragging tools—or I see a ladder leaning up in somebody’s yard, it sort of jumps right back into my head.\n\nFor a second it feels like I’m under there again, waiting for somebody to come help. Then I’ll look around and remember where I am. Funny how a sound can do that… my neighbor’s got this old aluminum ladder he leaves out by the fence, and I wish he’d put the thing away.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid thinking about the ladder accident or try to push memories of it out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. I don’t sit down and think, “I’m not gonna think about it,” but I keep busy with other things. I’ll find some reason not to go over to the senior center, or not to mess around in the garage.\n\nThe tools are out there, and that corner where I used to keep the ladder… well, I just don’t care to be near it much. I’ll tell myself I need to check the mail, or make coffee, or look for something in the kitchen. Funny thing, half the time I forget what I went in there for. But yes, I suppose I do push it away some.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the ladder accident as if it were happening all over again, without being triggered by a specific sound or sight?\n\nPatient: Not really just out of nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more when something sets it off, like that metal scraping sound, or seeing a ladder propped up in somebody’s yard.\n\nThen, for a moment, the whole thing comes back like I’m under there again, waiting for someone to help. But if there’s no sound or nothing I see… I don’t know, I can’t say it happens that way. Funny, though, how just one little scrape can do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know folks say things like that, like they’re looking down at themselves, but that’s not really how it is for me.\n\nIt’s more like I get startled and my mind goes back for a second, then I’m standing there in my kitchen or by the fence or wherever. I’m still me, still in my body, far as I can tell. Though, you know, my daughter once told me I looked pale as a sheet when I got spooked by a noise—funny expression, pale as a sheet. My mother used to say that all the time. But no, I haven’t noticed watching myself from outside or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or the way you've been feeling affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I suppose. My wife will be sitting right there next to me, and the fellows I play bridge with will be cutting up, telling jokes, you know how men do when they’ve known each other a while… and I feel like I’m behind a window or something.\n\nI can see them, hear them, but I don’t feel quite… with them. Like I’m there at the table, but not really part of it. Course, bridge is funny that way too, because if you miss a bid they’ll let you know about it—my friend Carl, he never lets a man forget a bad hand. But yes, I think it’s made me pull back some, even when I’m sitting right there with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or the world around you when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Detached from myself, or the world… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just quiet, sitting there, and my wife says I’m staring off, but I might just be thinking about whether I left the garage light on or what we need from the store.\n\nDetached is a funny word. Makes me think of when a trailer hitch isn’t set right—my brother-in-law had one come loose years ago, scared him half to death. But no, when I’m not thinking about the ladder thing, I can’t say I notice feeling separate from everything. I’m just… there, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from what's happening around you, even when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: A daze… well, I don’t know. I mean, I get tired sometimes, but at my age who doesn’t, right? My wife’ll ask me something when the television’s on and I might miss it, but that could just be my hearing. She says I keep the volume up too high, same as my father did.\n\nBut disconnected from what’s going on when I’m not thinking about that accident… no, I can’t say I’ve really noticed that. If I’m sitting there quiet, I’m usually just sitting there quiet. Maybe thinking on some little thing, like whether I shut the gate or what’s for supper. So… no, not in a way I’d call being in a daze.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities since the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t work anymore, not regular work anyhow. Haven’t for a while. But around the house… yes, I suppose things have changed some.\n\nI still get up, wash, eat breakfast, do my usual things. I’m not helpless. But there are chores I used to just do without thinking, and now I kind of steer around them, and then my wife ends up doing it. I don’t like that. She’s got enough on her plate.\n\nAnd I used to go down to the senior center more, play cards, talk with the fellas. They had terrible coffee there—tasted like it sat on the burner since Eisenhower—but I went. Lately I just don’t much. Same with my bridge friends. I don’t visit like I used to. So the days are… smaller, I guess. More just here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have interfered with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Significant… well, I guess it depends what you call significant. I still get up and do my regular things, you know, wash up, have breakfast, putter around. Not all the time, but enough that I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thoughts or memories about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I guess I do. It’s more when something sets it off, like that metal scraping sound, or seeing a ladder propped up in somebody’s yard.\n\nThen, for a moment, the whole thing comes back like I’m under there again, waiting for someone to help. But if there’s no sound or nothing I see… I don’t know, I can’t say it happens that way. Funny, though, how just one little scrape can do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Outside my body? No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know folks say things like that, like they’re looking down at themselves, but that’s not really how it is for me.\n\nIt’s more like I get startled and my mind goes back for a second, then I’m standing there in my kitchen or by the fence or wherever. I’m still me, still in my body, far as I can tell. Though, you know, my daughter once told me I looked pale as a sheet when I got spooked by a noise—funny expression, pale as a sheet. My mother used to say that all the time. But no, I haven’t noticed watching myself from outside or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or the way you've been feeling affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I suppose. My wife will be sitting right there next to me, and the fellows I play bridge with will be cutting up, telling jokes, you know how men do when they’ve known each other a while… and I feel like I’m behind a window or something.\n\nI can see them, hear them, but I don’t feel quite… with them. Like I’m there at the table, but not really part of it. Course, bridge is funny that way too, because if you miss a bid they’ll let you know about it—my friend Carl, he never lets a man forget a bad hand. But yes, I think it’s made me pull back some, even when I’m sitting right there with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself or the world around you when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Detached from myself, or the world… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just quiet, sitting there, and my wife says I’m staring off, but I might just be thinking about whether I left the garage light on or what we need from the store.\n\nDetached is a funny word. Makes me think of when a trailer hitch isn’t set right—my brother-in-law had one come loose years ago, scared him half to death. But no, when I’m not thinking about the ladder thing, I can’t say I notice feeling separate from everything. I’m just… there, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from what's happening around you, even when you're not thinking about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: A daze… well, I don’t know. I mean, I get tired sometimes, but at my age who doesn’t, right? My wife’ll ask me something when the television’s on and I might miss it, but that could just be my hearing. She says I keep the volume up too high, same as my father did.\n\nBut disconnected from what’s going on when I’m not thinking about that accident… no, I can’t say I’ve really noticed that. If I’m sitting there quiet, I’m usually just sitting there quiet. Maybe thinking on some little thing, like whether I shut the gate or what’s for supper. So… no, not in a way I’d call being in a daze.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities since the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Well, I don’t work anymore, not regular work anyhow. Haven’t for a while. But around the house… yes, I suppose things have changed some.\n\nI still get up, wash, eat breakfast, do my usual things. I’m not helpless. But there are chores I used to just do without thinking, and now I kind of steer around them, and then my wife ends up doing it. I don’t like that. She’s got enough on her plate.\n\nAnd I used to go down to the senior center more, play cards, talk with the fellas. They had terrible coffee there—tasted like it sat on the burner since Eisenhower—but I went. Lately I just don’t much. Same with my bridge friends. I don’t visit like I used to. So the days are… smaller, I guess. More just here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have interfered with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Significant… well, I guess it depends what you call significant. I still get up and do my regular things, you know, wash up, have breakfast, putter around. I’m not just sitting in a chair all day like my uncle did after his hip went bad—though he was stubborn as a mule about that walker.\n\nBut yes, it’s changed things some. I don’t go over to the senior center like I used to, and I hardly see the bridge fellas anymore. Around the house, there are things I avoid, and then my wife ends up doing chores that used to be mine. I don’t feel too good about that.\n\nSo… I suppose my days have gotten smaller. More just me at home, and her picking up some of what I don’t do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently avoiding thoughts, conversations, or memories related to the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I don’t sit there saying to myself, “Don’t think about it,” exactly. It’s more like I find some other reason not to get near it.\n\nLike the senior center—I keep meaning to go, then I say, ah, it’s too cold, or I don’t feel like hearing all the chatter today. And the garage too. I’ll tell myself it needs tidying but then I won’t go work around in there, especially where the tools are, and that spot where I used to keep the ladder.\n\nFunny thing, I still know where every wrench is supposed to go. My father had a pegboard in his shed, and if you put the pliers back wrong, he’d notice before supper. But… anyway, yes. I guess I do avoid things that bring it back. Not all the time, but enough that I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thoughts or memories about the ladder accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I guess I do. I don’t always call it that in my head, but I keep finding reasons not to go over to the senior center, or not to work around the garage.\n\nThe garage especially… near the tools and that spot where I used to keep the ladder. I’ll say, “Well, I’ll do it tomorrow,” or “It’s too much mess today.” Funny, I used to keep that place pretty tidy—had coffee cans full of screws, all sorted out. But now I just sort of… stay away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are causing you a lot of distress or significantly interfering with your life?\n\nPatient: Well… a lot of distress, I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. I mean, I still get myself washed up, eat breakfast, do my little routine. I’m not helpless.\n\nBut it has interfered, yes. I don’t go to the senior center like I did, and I hardly see the bridge fellows anymore. We used to sit there for hours arguing over bids—one man, Harold, always thought he was smarter than the rest of us. Anyway… I miss that more than I admit.\n\nAnd around the house, I avoid enough things that my wife ends up doing chores I used to handle. That bothers me. So I guess my life’s gotten smaller, more just staying home and letting things slide."
  }
]
